 |\partial_u^a\partial_x^b w_{j,x}(u)|
   &\le C_r(k/\delta_j)^r\delta_j^k
                  &&(1\le j\le J,\ u\in\mathbb R), \label{weights:short-derivatives}\\
 |\partial_u^a\partial_x^b w_{0,x}(u)|
   &\le C_r k^r
                  &&(u\ge-\log2/\ell). \label{weights:main-derivatives}
\end{aligned}$$ There is also $c>0$, independent of $r,k,X$, such that, with $\delta=\delta_J$ and $n=e^{\ell u}>0$, $$|\partial_u^a\partial_x^b w_{*,x}(u)|
       \le C_r(k/\delta)^r\delta^k e^{-cn/X}.
 \label{weights:terminal-derivatives}$$ Changing the fixed compact neighborhood of $\mathcal K$ changes only the constants.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-weights-derivatives"><summary>Proof of stmt-weights-derivatives</summary>

<a id="weights:power-derivative"></a>
*Proof.* It is convenient first to differentiate using $D_x=x\partial_x$. For $$h=1-u+\ell^{-1}\log(C_F|x|v)$$ and $a+b\le r$, differentiation under the integral gives $$\partial_u^aD_x^b h_+^k
    =(-1)^a\ell^{-b}(k)_{a+b}h_+^{k-a-b},
 \label{weights:power-derivative}$$ where $(k)_t=k(k-1)\cdots(k-t+1)$. The choice $k\ge r+2$ ensures enough continuous derivatives at $h=0$; domination for differentiating follows from the estimates below. Ordinary $x$-derivatives are fixed linear combinations of $x^{-b}D_x^i$, $i\le b$, and therefore satisfy the same bounds on our compact set.

Suppose $1-u\le\delta'$, where $\delta'>0$. For $0\le t\le r$, write $m=k-t>0$ and $a_t=m/(\ell\delta')$. The elementary inequality $(1+q)_+^m\le e^{mq}$, valid for every real $q$, gives <a id="weights:gamma-majorant"></a>$$h_+^m
    \le(\delta')^m(C_F|x|v)^{a_t}.
 \label{weights:gamma-majorant}$$ If $\delta'=\delta_j$, then $0<a_t\le\ell^{-3/10}$ by <a href="#weights:scale-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="weights:scale-bounds">[weights:scale-bounds]</a>, so $$\int_0^\infty e^{-v}(C_F|x|v)^{a_t}\,dv
       =(C_F|x|)^{a_t}\Gamma(1+a_t)\le C.$$ The bound is uniform once $X$ is large enough that $a_t\le1$. Thus, on $1-u\le\delta_j$, a derivative of $W_{k,x}$ involving at most $r$ differentiations is bounded by a constant times $k^r\delta_j^{k-r}$. The cutoff derivatives of order $q$ cost $O_r(\delta_j^{-q})$. More precisely, each Leibniz term with $t$ derivatives hitting $W$ and $q$ hitting a cutoff has $t+q\le r$, and is at most $$C_r k^t\delta_j^{k-t-q}
       \le C_r(k/\delta_j)^r\delta_j^k.$$ This proves <a href="#weights:short-derivatives" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="weights:short-derivatives">[weights:short-derivatives]</a>.

For $u\ge-\log2/\ell$, use instead $\delta'=1+\log2/\ell$. Now $(\delta')^k\le\exp(k\log2/\ell)\le2$ for large $X$, and $a_t\le k/\ell\le\ell^{-2/5}$. The same argument and the fixed derivative bounds for $F_0$ prove <a href="#weights:main-derivatives" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="weights:main-derivatives">[weights:main-derivatives]</a>.

On the support of the terminal cutoff, or any nonzero derivative of it, $1-u\le\delta_J/2\le\delta_J$. Moreover $h>0$ implies $$v>\frac{n}{C_F|x|X}.$$ Retain half the exponential in the integral. Uniformly on our compact set, <a id="weights:retained-exponential"></a>$$\begin{split}
 \int_{\{h>0\}}e^{-v}(C_F|x|v)^{a_t}\,dv
 &\le e^{-n/(2C_F|x|X)}
       (C_F|x|)^{a_t}
       \int_0^\infty e^{-v/2}v^{a_t}\,dv\\
 &=e^{-n/(2C_F|x|X)}
       (C_F|x|)^{a_t}2^{1+a_t}\Gamma(1+a_t)
 \le C e^{-cn/X}.
 \end{split}
 \label{weights:retained-exponential}$$ Here $a_t=(k-t)/(\ell\delta_J)\le\ell^{-3/10}$, so $2^{1+a_t}\Gamma(1+a_t)$ is bounded independently of $k$. The same Leibniz calculation now proves <a href="#weights:terminal-derivatives" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="weights:terminal-derivatives">[weights:terminal-derivatives]</a>. All integrands used for differentiation are dominated on compact sets by the displayed integrable majorants, which also justifies <a href="#weights:power-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="weights:power-derivative">[weights:power-derivative]</a> under the integral. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-weights-comparison-interface"></a>
<div id="weights:comparison-interface" class="corollary">

<a id="weights:Ax"></a>
**Corollary 15** (Comparison interface). *For every fixed derivative order $r$, the short weights satisfy the smoothness assumptions of Proposition <a href="#analytic:comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="analytic:comparison">12</a> with derivative bounds $\ell^{C_r}$, uniformly in $j,k,X$. They have length $X^{1-\Delta_j}$, with $$\Delta_j=\delta_j/4,\qquad
 \tfrac14\ell^{-1/10}\le\Delta_j\le\tfrac14 .$$ The deterministic term $$s=k/\ell,\qquad A_x=(C_F|x|)^s\Gamma(1+s)
 \label{weights:Ax}$$ has uniformly bounded derivatives of each fixed order on $\mathcal K$, and is bounded above and below by positive constants. The endpoint enlargement at $n=1$ can be made inside the fixed range $n\ge1/2$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-weights-comparison-interface"><summary>Proof of stmt-weights-comparison-interface</summary>

*Proof.* The bounds $k\le\ell^{3/5}$ and $\delta_j^{-1}\le\ell^{1/10}$ make <a href="#weights:short-derivatives" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="weights:short-derivatives">[weights:short-derivatives]</a> polynomial in $\ell$. Choose a fixed smooth function $\chi$ equal to zero on $(-\infty,-\log2]$ and equal to one on $[0,\infty)$. For the comparison alone, replace $w_{0,x}(u)$ on the real line by $$\widetilde w_{0,x}(u)=\chi(\ell u)w_{0,x}(u).$$ It agrees with the original weight at every positive integer $n=e^{\ell u}$, and its extra derivatives cost only fixed powers of $\ell$, by <a href="#weights:main-derivatives" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="weights:main-derivatives">[weights:main-derivatives]</a>. Its left support is $u\ge-\log2/\ell$, exactly the enlargement $n\ge1/2$. For $j\ge1$, the weights already vanish on a neighborhood of $u\le0$. After a fixed smooth dyadic localization $n=Ny$, the operator $y\partial_y$ acts as $\ell^{-1}\partial_u$, so the same bounds give all the required seminorms in the scaled variables. The endpoint extension has therefore taken place in $\log n$; no extension to a fixed negative value of $u$ is used.

Finally $0<s\le\ell^{-2/5}$, while $C_F|x|$ lies in a fixed positive compact interval. The formula for $A_x$, its derivatives in $x$, and the continuity and positivity of $\Gamma(1+s)$ give the assertions concerning $A_x$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
#### The terminal mean square

<a id="weights:moment-interface"></a>
We use the following consequence of Proposition <a href="#analytic:polynomial-moment" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="analytic:polynomial-moment">7</a>. For general $X$, cover $[X,2X)$ by at most two dyadic blocks whose heights are comparable to $X$; the normalization and polynomial factors in that proposition then change by fixed constants only. For any fixed smooth compactly supported function $G$ on $(0,\infty)$, there are fixed exponents $A,B$ such that $$\frac1X\sum_{\substack{d\ {\rm admissible}\\X\le|d|<2X}}
 \left|\sum_{n\ge1}\frac{\lambda(n)\chi_d(n)}{\sqrt n}\,
                         n^{-it}G(n/N)\right|^2
 \ll_G \ell^B(1+|t|)^A(1+N/X)^A.
 \label{weights:moment-interface}$$ Its applicability includes the fixed enlarging bump used below.

<a id="stmt-weights-terminal"></a>
<div id="weights:terminal" class="proposition">

<a id="weights:terminal-square"></a>
**Proposition 16** (Terminal mean square). *There is a fixed exponent $C_T$, chosen before $k_0$, such that uniformly in <a href="#weights:range" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="weights:range">[weights:range]</a>, $$\frac1X\sum_{\substack{d\ {\rm admissible}\\X\le|d|<2X}}
       |S_{*,\chi_d}(d/X)|^2
       \ll \ell^{C_T}\delta_J^{2k}.
 \label{weights:terminal-square}$$ The same estimate holds after restricting the summation to either value of $\chi_d(2)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-weights-terminal"><summary>Proof of stmt-weights-terminal</summary>

<a id="weights:terminal-kernel"></a>
*Proof.* Choose a smooth dyadic partition with a fixed $\rho\in C_c^\infty((1/2,2))$ satisfying $$\sum_{N\in\{1,2,4,\ldots\}}\rho(n/N)=1\qquad(n\ge1).$$ For example one may start with a smooth nonincreasing function equal to one on $(0,1]$ and zero on $[2,\infty)$, and take its difference at arguments $y$ and $2y$; choosing its transition strictly inside $(1,2)$ gives the asserted compact support. Fix also $G\in C_c^\infty((0,\infty))$ equal to one on $\operatorname{supp}\rho$. Put $$T_N(y,x)=\rho(y)w_{*,x}(\ell^{-1}\log(Ny)),\qquad
 \widehat T_N(t,x)=\int_0^\infty T_N(y,x)y^{it}\frac{dy}{y}.$$ Select once and for all integers $r>A/2+2$ and $D>A/2+2$, and then choose $k_0\ge r+2$. If derivatives required elsewhere are of higher order, increase this fixed $r$ first. By Lemma <a href="#weights:derivatives" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="weights:derivatives">14</a>, differentiation on the $y$-scale through order $r$ gives $$\left|(y\partial_y)^a T_N(y,x)\right|
       \ll_r \ell^{C_r}\delta_J^k e^{-c'N/X}
       \qquad(0\le a\le r),$$ uniformly in $x\in\mathcal K$. There is no boundary term when integrating by parts in $\log y$. Consequently, $$\sup_{x\in\mathcal K}|\widehat T_N(t,x)|
   \ll_{r,D}\ell^{C_r}\delta_J^k
       (1+N/X)^{-D}(1+|t|)^{-r}.
 \label{weights:terminal-kernel}$$ In passing from the exponential to $(1+N/X)^{-D}$, the constant depends on the fixed $D$, not on $k$.

Mellin inversion now expresses the $N$-piece of the terminal sum as $$\frac1{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb R}
   \widehat T_N(t,d/X)N^{it}
   P_d(N,t)\,dt,\qquad
 P_d(N,t)=\sum_{n\ge1}\frac{\lambda(n)\chi_d(n)}{\sqrt n}\,
                        n^{-it}G(n/N).$$ Indeed $G(n/N)T_N(n/N,x)=T_N(n/N,x)$. Define the normalized family norm by $$\|B_d\|_{2,X}
   =\left(\frac1X\sum_{\substack{d\ {\rm admissible}\\X\le|d|<2X}}
                      |B_d|^2\right)^{1/2}.$$ We bound the kernel uniformly before applying the family moment. For each fixed $t$, $$\|\widehat T_N(t,d/X)P_d(N,t)\|_{2,X}
   \le \sup_{x\in\mathcal K}|\widehat T_N(t,x)|
                           \|P_d(N,t)\|_{2,X}.$$ Minkowski’s inequality, followed by <a href="#weights:moment-interface" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="weights:moment-interface">[weights:moment-interface]</a> and <a href="#weights:terminal-kernel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="weights:terminal-kernel">[weights:terminal-kernel]</a>, therefore yields $$\begin{split}
 \|S_{*,\chi_d}(d/X)\|_{2,X}
 &\ll \ell^{C_r+B/2}\delta_J^k
   \sum_{N=1,2,4,\ldots}(1+N/X)^{-D+A/2}
   \int_{\mathbb R}(1+|t|)^{-r+A/2}\,dt\\
 &\ll \ell^{C_r+B/2+1}\delta_J^k.
 \end{split}$$ The integral converges by the fixed choice of $r$. There are $O(\ell)$ dyadic terms with $N\le X$, and those with $N>X$ form a convergent geometric tail by the choice of $D$. All sums and integrals can first be truncated; the displayed integrable majorants justify passage to the limit. Squaring proves <a href="#weights:terminal-square" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="weights:terminal-square">[weights:terminal-square]</a>, for example with $C_T=2C_r+B+2$. This exponent depends only on choices made before $k_0$. Restricting the family can only decrease the nonnegative sum. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-weights-terminal-small"></a>
<div id="weights:terminal-small" class="lemma">

<a id="weights:comparison-absorption"></a>
**Lemma 17** (Uniform absorption of the terminal loss). *After increasing the fixed $k_0$, one sufficiently large lower bound on $X$ makes <a id="weights:terminal-small-square"></a>$$\frac1X\sum_{\substack{d\ {\rm admissible}\\X\le|d|<2X}}
       |S_{*,\chi_d}(d/X)|^2
       \ll 2^{-3Jk/2}
 \label{weights:terminal-small-square}$$ simultaneously for all $k$ in <a href="#weights:range" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="weights:range">[weights:range]</a>. Moreover, for any fixed $c,L>0$, the comparison errors obey $$e^{-c\Delta_j\ell}
       \le k^{-L}2^{-Ljk}\qquad(0\le j\le J)
 \label{weights:comparison-absorption}$$ for one sufficiently large lower bound on $X$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-weights-terminal-small"><summary>Proof of stmt-weights-terminal-small</summary>

*Proof.* The ratio of the right side of <a href="#weights:terminal-square" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="weights:terminal-square">[weights:terminal-square]</a> to $2^{-3Jk/2}$, apart from its fixed implied constant, is $$\ell^{C_T}2^{-Jk/2}.$$ For $J\ge1$ this decreases with $k$, so throughout the permitted range it is at most its value at the fixed integer $k_0$. The floor in the definition of $J$ gives $$\ell^{C_T}2^{-Jk/2}
       \le \ell^{C_T}2^{-Jk_0/2}
       \le 2^{k_0/2}\ell^{C_T-k_0/20}.$$ Choose $k_0>20C_T$ after $C_T$ is fixed. The last expression then tends to zero with $X$. In particular the choice of a single $X$-threshold works for the entire $k$-range.

For the second assertion, $\Delta_j\ell\ge\ell^{9/10}/4$, whereas uniformly in the same range $$L\log k+Ljk\log2
       =O_L(\ell^{3/5}\log\ell)=o(\ell^{9/10}).$$ Taking logarithms proves <a href="#weights:comparison-absorption" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="weights:comparison-absorption">[weights:comparison-absorption]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:model"></a>
### Block mollifiers and independent-model mean squares

<span id="model:section" label="model:section"></span>

We seek polynomial multipliers that make the first short piece close to the positive quantity $A_x$ from <a href="#weights:Ax" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="weights:Ax">[weights:Ax]</a>, while keeping the later pieces small. The construction has two separate tasks: control the multipliers on most integers, and prove mean-square bounds on the entire independent model. Their combination will allow the first piece to dominate the weighted-series identity.

Fix a member $F$ of the finite family $\mathcal F$ and a value $c\in\{-1,1\}$ at the prime $2$, and write $\lambda=\lambda_F$ and $\xi_p=\xi_{F,p}$. The constants in this section are uniform in these two choices. We use the integer characters $z_m$ and the independent variables $Z$ defined above; in particular, at an odd prime $$\mathbb P(Z(p)=0)=p^{-1},\qquad
 \mathbb P(Z(p)=1)=\mathbb P(Z(p)=-1)=(1-p^{-1})/2,
 \qquad Z(2)=c.$$ All model norms below are norms on this entire probability space. We use the parameters and weights of Section <a href="#sec:weights" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:weights">4</a>: $$\ell=\log X,\qquad k_0\le k\le\ell^{3/5},\qquad
 J=\left\lfloor\frac{\log\ell}{10\log2}\right\rfloor,
 \qquad \delta_j=2^{-j},\qquad s=k/\ell.$$ Here $k$ is an integer. We always take $X$ sufficiently large that $J\ge1$ and $0<s\le1$. The scaled variable $x$ ranges over a fixed compact subset $\mathcal X$ of $\mathbb R\setminus\{0\}$ containing the support of the averaging weight. Constants may depend on this compact set, the fixed cutoff $f_*$, and $\mathcal F$.

The shift $s$ comes from the weight of the first piece. Replacing the truncated power in <a href="#weights:W" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="weights:W">[weights:W]</a> by its exponential reference gives the exact integral $$\int_0^\infty e^{-v}
       \exp\bigl(-ku+s\log(C_F|x|v)\bigr)\,dv
       =A_xe^{-ku}.$$ At $u=\log n/\ell$, the reference weight is therefore $A_xn^{-s}$. This suggests a multiplier approximating the inverse Euler product at the real shift $s$. Lemma <a href="#model:first-Fourier-error" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="model:first-Fourier-error">25</a> and the final Fourier argument will quantify the replacement in the required norm; the reference calculation alone does not replace the weighted series.

<a id="pm-h-20"></a>
#### Elementary prime estimates and local factors

<a id="model:prime-mass"></a>
<a id="model:prime-tail"></a>
<a id="model:prime-transition"></a>
We shall repeatedly use the elementary estimate $\pi(y)\ll y/\log(2y)$. One proof starts with $$\sum_{n<p\le2n}\log p\le\log\binom{2n}{n}\le2n\log2.$$ Summing on dyadic intervals gives $\sum_{p\le y}\log p\ll y$; separating $p\le\sqrt y$ then proves the asserted estimate for $\pi(y)$. Partial summation gives the following consequences, with absolute constants: $$\begin{aligned}
 \sum_{p\le y}\frac1p&\ll1+\log\log(3y),\nonumber\\
 \sum_{a<p\le b}\frac1p&\ll
 1+\log\frac{\log b}{\log a}\quad(2\le a\le b),
 \label{model:prime-mass}\\
 \sum_{p>Y}p^{-1-2a}
 &\ll\int_{2a\log Y}^{\infty}\frac{e^{-v}}v\,dv
 \ll 1+\log^+\frac1{a\log Y}\qquad(0<a\le1,\ Y\ge2).
 \label{model:prime-tail}
\end{aligned}$$ Here $\log^+t=\max(0,\log t)$. If $a\log Y\ge1/2$, we also have $$\int_{2a\log Y}^{\infty}\frac{e^{-v}}v\,dv
 \ll\frac{e^{-2a\log Y}}{a\log Y}.$$ For clarity, the first bound in <a href="#model:prime-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:prime-tail">[model:prime-tail]</a> follows by integrating $t^{-1-2a}$ against $d\pi(t)$, discarding the nonpositive boundary term at $Y$, and using $1+2a\le3$. We also need, for $0<a\le1$ and $h\ge a$, $$\sum_p\frac{\min(1,h\log p)}p p^{-2a}
 \ll 1+\log(1+h/a).
 \label{model:prime-transition}$$ Here is a direct verification. If $h\le1$, primes with $\log p\le1/h$ contribute at most $$h\sum_{p\le e^{1/h}}\frac{\log p}{p}\ll1.$$ Between $1/h$ and $1/a$ the reciprocal-prime mass is $O(1+\log(h/a))$ by <a href="#model:prime-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:prime-mass">[model:prime-mass]</a>; primes beyond $e^{1/a}$ contribute $O(1)$ by <a href="#model:prime-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:prime-tail">[model:prime-tail]</a>. Empty ranges are omitted. If $h>1$, discard the minimum and use the same estimates to obtain $O(1+\log(1/a))$, which is bounded by the right side of <a href="#model:prime-transition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:prime-transition">[model:prime-transition]</a>.

<a id="model:local-bounds"></a>
For any real character value $V(p)\in\{-1,0,1\}$ define <a id="model:inverse-local"></a>$$D_p(b,V)=1-\lambda(p)V(p)p^{-1/2-b}
                +\xi_p V(p)^2p^{-1-2b},\qquad \Re b\ge0.
 \label{model:inverse-local}$$ The local parameter bound gives a factorization $D_p(b,V)=\prod_{\nu=1}^2(1-\alpha_{p,\nu}V(p)p^{-1/2-b})$, where $|\alpha_{p,\nu}|\le1$ and zero parameters are permitted. Consequently $$(1-p^{-1/2})^2\le |D_p(b,V)|\le(1+p^{-1/2})^2.
 \label{model:local-bounds}$$ For real $b\ge0$, the value of $D_p(b,V)$ is positive: at a good prime its two factors are complex conjugates (or both positive real), and at a bad prime its nontrivial factor is positive. These facts apply equally to $V=Z$ and $V=z_m$.

<a id="pm-h-21"></a>
#### Construction, length, and the simultaneous product event

Radziwiłł and Soundararajan use disjoint prime blocks, truncated exponential polynomials, and relative truncation estimates in their study of central values of quadratic twists (Radziwiłł and Soundararajan 2015, secs. 3, 8, and 9) <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[RadziwillSoundararajan2015](#ref-RadziwillSoundararajan2015)]. Here we truncate products of inverse local Euler factors by total degree and estimate shifted norms over the full integer model defined in Section <a href="#sec:setup" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:setup">2</a>.

<a id="model:block-mass"></a>
Put $$Y_j=X^{\delta_j^2/\sqrt k},\qquad
 P_j^{\mathrm{eu}}(V)=\prod_{p\le Y_j}D_p(s,V)^{-1}
 \quad(0\le j\le J).$$ Since $s\log Y_0=\sqrt k$, the prime-tail estimate <a href="#model:prime-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:prime-tail">[model:prime-tail]</a> makes $\sum_{p>Y_0}p^{-1-2s}$ exponentially small. This sum will control the residual Euler product in mean square. For the later pieces, the available multiplier length decreases as their lengths approach $X$. The smaller cutoffs $Y_j$ will keep the truncated products within the comparison allowance $X^{\eta\delta_j/4}$. In the estimates for these later pieces, the weight factor $\delta_j^k$ will absorb fixed polynomial losses in $k$ and $2^j$. In the stated range $\log Y_J\ge\ell^{1/2}$, so every $Y_j$ exceeds $2$ for a single sufficiently large $X$ threshold. For $i\ge0$ let $$I_{ji}=\{p:Y_j^{2^{-i-1}}<p\le Y_j^{2^{-i}}\},\qquad
 m_{ji}=\lceil k^{1/4}+j+i\rceil.$$ We omit empty blocks. They form a disjoint partition of the primes at most $Y_j$, and <a href="#model:prime-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:prime-mass">[model:prime-mass]</a> gives $$\sum_{p\in I_{ji}}p^{-1}\le C
 \label{model:block-mass}$$ uniformly in $j,i,k,X$. Indeed, when the lower endpoint is at least $2$, the ratio of the logarithms of the endpoints is $2$; otherwise a nonempty block has upper endpoint less than $4$. Set <a id="model:mollifier-definition"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 K_{ji}(V,z)&=\prod_{p\in I_{ji}}
  \left(1-\lambda(p)V(p)p^{-1/2-s}z
          +\xi_pV(p)^2p^{-1-2s}z^2\right),
 \nonumber\\
 T_{ji}(V)&=\sum_{b=0}^{m_{ji}}[z^b]K_{ji}(V,z),
 &H_j(V)&=\prod_i T_{ji}(V).
 \label{model:mollifier-definition}
\end{aligned}$$ The coefficient extraction in this definition is followed by evaluation at $z=1$.

<a id="stmt-model-mollifier-length"></a>
<div id="model:mollifier-length" class="lemma">

<a id="model:length-bound"></a>
**Lemma 18** (Length and coefficients). *The polynomial $H_j$ has an expansion $$H_j(V)=\sum_{r\le R_j}h_j(r)V(r),\qquad
 |h_j(r)|\le\tau(r)r^{-1/2},\qquad
 \sum_r|h_j(r)|\ll R_j^2,$$ where, for a fixed constant $C_{\rm len}>0$, one may take $$R_j=\exp\left(C_{\rm len}(k^{1/4}+j+1)
                     \ell\delta_j^2/\sqrt k\right).
 \label{model:length-bound}$$ For every fixed $\eta>0$, a sufficiently large fixed $k_0$ ensures $R_j\le X^{\eta\delta_j/4}$ for all $0\le j\le J$ and all $k$ in the stated range. Thus $H_j$ satisfies the multiplier hypotheses of Proposition <a href="#analytic:comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="analytic:comparison">12</a>, with $\Delta=\delta_j/4$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-model-mollifier-length"><summary>Proof of stmt-model-mollifier-length</summary>

*Proof.* In a block, a chosen monomial has prime exponents $e_p\in\{0,1,2\}$ whose sum is at most $m_{ji}$. Its integer index is therefore at most $\exp(m_{ji}2^{-i}\log Y_j)$. Since $$\sum_{i\ge0}m_{ji}2^{-i}
 \le\sum_{i\ge0}(k^{1/4}+j+i+1)2^{-i}
 =2k^{1/4}+2j+4,$$ we obtain <a href="#model:length-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:length-bound">[model:length-bound]</a>. Unique factorization shows that each index occurs in exactly one prime-exponent pattern. The truncations only delete patterns. A surviving coefficient is a product of local coefficients $1$, $-\lambda(p)p^{-1/2-s}$, or $\xi_pp^{-1-2s}$, and hence is bounded by $\tau(r)r^{-1/2-s}\le\tau(r)r^{-1/2}$. For example, $$\sum_{r\le R}\tau(r)r^{-1/2}
 =\sum_{ab\le R}(ab)^{-1/2}
 \le2\sqrt R\sum_{a\le R}\frac1a
 \ll\sqrt R\log(2R)\ll R^2\qquad(R\ge1).$$ Finally, $$\frac{\log R_j}{\ell\delta_j/4}
 \le4C_{\rm len}\bigl(k^{-1/4}+(j+1)2^{-j}k^{-1/2}\bigr)
 \le8C_{\rm len}k^{-1/4}.$$ Here $(j+1)2^{-j}\le1$. Thus the fixed choice $k_0\ge(8C_{\rm len}/\eta)^4$ suffices simultaneously for all $j$. The coefficient estimate and this length bound verify both multiplier conditions of Proposition <a href="#analytic:comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="analytic:comparison">12</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-model-integer-moments"></a>
<div id="model:integer-moments" class="lemma">

<a id="model:64th-moment"></a>
**Lemma 19** (Prime-sum moments for the integer model). *Let $\mathcal I$ be any set of primes at most $Y_0$, and put $$B_{\mathcal I}(V)=\sum_{p\in\mathcal I}
                 \lambda(p)V(p)p^{-1/2-s}.$$ If $k_0$ is a sufficiently large absolute constant, then $$\frac1X\sum_{X\le|m|<2X}|B_{\mathcal I}(z_m)|^{64}
 \ll\left(1+\sum_{p\in\mathcal I}\frac1p\right)^{32}
 \label{model:64th-moment}$$ uniformly in $\mathcal I,k,X$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-model-integer-moments"><summary>Proof of stmt-model-integer-moments</summary>

*Proof.* First omit the deterministic prime $2$ and write $a_p=\lambda(p)p^{-1/2-s}$. On expanding the model moment, any odd prime that occurs just once has zero expectation. Group the remaining terms by their partition of the $64$ positions into $q\le32$ parts of sizes at least two. Since $|a_p|\le2$, for every part of size $r\ge2$ we have $|a_p|^r\le2^{r-2}|a_p|^2$. Summing the distinct-prime choices and then discarding distinctness bounds the moment by $$C\sum_{q=1}^{32}\left(\sum_{p\in\mathcal I}|a_p|^2\right)^q
 \ll\left(1+\sum_{p\in\mathcal I}\frac1p\right)^{32}.$$ Restoring the prime $2$ changes only the constant, by $|u+v|^{64}\le2^{63}(|u|^{64}+|v|^{64})$ and $|a_2c|\le\sqrt2$.

For completeness the passage to integers needs no estimate for primes in progressions. For each ordered $64$-tuple of primes, the product of its character values is periodic modulo the product of its odd primes, which is at most $Y_0^{64}$. Its absolute value is at most one. The arithmetic mean over the two integer intervals $X\le|m|<2X$ differs from its complete-residue mean by $O(Y_0^{64}/X)$. The complete-residue mean is exactly the independent-model mean, by the Chinese remainder theorem and the counts of $0,1,-1$ for the Legendre symbol. There are at most $Y_0^{64}$ tuples and each coefficient has absolute value at most $2^{64}$. Thus the discrepancy between that arithmetic mean and the model moment is $$O(Y_0^{128}/X)=O(X^{-1+128/\sqrt k})=O(X^{-1/2})$$ after requiring $\sqrt{k_0}\ge256$. Since the number of integers in these intervals is $O(X)$, this proves <a href="#model:64th-moment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:64th-moment">[model:64th-moment]</a> with its normalization by $X$. If endpoints are not integers, the same period-counting argument changes only its absolute constant. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-model-good-event"></a>
<div id="model:good-event" class="proposition">

<a id="model:good-mollifier"></a>
<a id="model:good-ratio"></a>
<a id="model:good-full-product"></a>
**Proposition 20** (Simultaneous product event). *Except for $O(Xk^{-2})$ integers in $X\le|m|<2X$, the following inequalities hold simultaneously: $$\begin{aligned}
 \frac12\le H_j(z_m)P_j^{\mathrm{eu}}(z_m)&\le2
                         &&(0\le j\le J),
 \label{model:good-mollifier}\\
 \frac{P_0^{\mathrm{eu}}(z_m)}{P_j^{\mathrm{eu}}(z_m)}
   &\ge2^{-jk/8} &&(1\le j\le J),
 \label{model:good-ratio}\\
 P_0^{\mathrm{eu}}(z_m)&\ge2^{-Jk/8}.
 \label{model:good-full-product}
\end{aligned}$$ The implied constant and the lower threshold $k_0$ do not depend on $k,j,X$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-model-good-event"><summary>Proof of stmt-model-good-event</summary>

*Proof.* For a block $I=I_{ji}$ write $B=B_I(z_m)$. The elementary expansion $\log(1+w)=w+O(|w|^2)$ at all sufficiently large primes, together with <a href="#model:block-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:block-mass">[model:block-mass]</a>, gives <a id="model:pointwise-block"></a>$$\sup_{|z|=2}|K_{ji}(z_m,z)|\le e^{2|B|+C},
 \qquad K_{ji}(z_m,1)\ge e^{-|B|-C}.
 \label{model:pointwise-block}$$ The finitely many smaller primes are included using <a href="#model:local-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:local-bounds">[model:local-bounds]</a> at $z=1$ and the direct upper bound $\prod_{\nu}(1+2p^{-1/2})$ on $|z|=2$; they change $C$ by an absolute amount. Thus no nonvanishing assertion on the radius-two circle is required. Cauchy’s coefficient estimate now gives, with $m=m_{ji}$, $$\frac{|T_{ji}(z_m)-K_{ji}(z_m,1)|}{K_{ji}(z_m,1)}
 \le C2^{-m}e^{3|B|}.$$ If every block satisfies $|B_{I_{ji}}(z_m)|\le m_{ji}/100$, the last quantity is at most $Ce^{-c m_{ji}}$ for a fixed $c>0$. The sum over $i$ is $O(e^{-c(k^{1/4}+j)})$. By enlarging $k_0$, the product over blocks of the ratios $T_{ji}(z_m)/K_{ji}(z_m,1)$ lies between $1/2$ and $2$. Since $\prod_iK_{ji}(z_m,1)=(P_j^{\mathrm{eu}}(z_m))^{-1}$, this proves <a href="#model:good-mollifier" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:good-mollifier">[model:good-mollifier]</a> on the specified event. The number excluded, divided by $X$, is at most $$C\sum_{j=0}^J\sum_{i\ge0}(k^{1/4}+j+i)^{-64}
 \ll (k^{1/4})^{-62}=k^{-31/2}\ll k^{-2},$$ by Lemma <a href="#model:integer-moments" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="model:integer-moments">19</a> and <a href="#model:block-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:block-mass">[model:block-mass]</a>. The sum may be extended to all $i$ because only nonempty blocks impose an event.

<a id="model:log-product"></a>
The real logarithm is available by positivity, and the same local expansion, including the finitely many small primes separately, gives for every prime set $\mathcal I$ $$\log\prod_{p\in\mathcal I}D_p(s,z_m)^{-1}
 =B_{\mathcal I}(z_m)+O\left(\sum_{p\in\mathcal I}p^{-1}\right).
 \label{model:log-product}$$ For $Y_j<p\le Y_0$, <a href="#model:prime-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:prime-mass">[model:prime-mass]</a> bounds this reciprocal mass by $C(1+j)$, since $\log Y_0/\log Y_j=4^j$. For $p\le Y_0$ it is at most $C(1+J)$: indeed $\log\log Y_0\le\log\ell$, and $\log\ell<10(J+1)\log2$. Require that the corresponding prime sums have absolute values at most $(\log2)jk/16$ and $(\log2)Jk/16$, respectively. Once $k_0$ dominates the fixed drift constants in <a href="#model:log-product" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:log-product">[model:log-product]</a>, these requirements imply <a href="#model:good-ratio" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:good-ratio">[model:good-ratio]</a> and <a href="#model:good-full-product" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:good-full-product">[model:good-full-product]</a>. Their total exceptional proportion is at most $$C\sum_{j=1}^J\frac{(1+j)^{32}}{(jk)^{64}}
   +C\frac{(1+J)^{32}}{(Jk)^{64}}
 \ll k^{-64}.$$ Combining the exceptional sets proves the proposition. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
#### Euler-product norms on the full model

The preceding proposition controls the multipliers on most integers. We now prove the model norms needed for arithmetic comparison, including the contribution of every event. We first estimate exact Euler products, and then replace each complete block by its degree truncation with a summable relative error.

<a id="model:finite-expansion"></a>
To avoid any limiting interchange, fix for now a finite real cutoff $Q\ge Y_0$ and put <a id="model:finite-Euler"></a>$$\mathcal L_Q(b)=\prod_{p\le Q}D_p(b,Z)^{-1},\qquad \Re b>0.
 \label{model:finite-Euler}$$ All bounds in this subsection are independent of $Q$. At fixed $Q$ the product has the absolutely convergent expansion $$\mathcal L_Q(b)=
 \sum_{\substack{n\ge1\\p\mid n\Rightarrow p\le Q}}
       \lambda(n)Z(n)n^{-1/2-b}.
 \label{model:finite-expansion}$$ Absolute convergence follows by multiplying the finitely many local geometric series; their absolute sums are bounded by $\prod_{p\le Q}(1-p^{-1/2-\Re b})^{-2}$.

<a id="stmt-model-exact-shifts"></a>
<div id="model:exact-shifts" class="lemma">

<a id="model:general-exact-norm"></a>
<a id="model:exact-distance"></a>
**Lemma 21** (Exact shifted products). *Let $0<s,\alpha\le1$, $b=\alpha+it$, and $Y_j\ge2$. If $h=\alpha+s+|t|$ and $a=\min(\alpha,s)$, then $$\|(P_j^{\mathrm{eu}})^{-1}\mathcal L_Q(b)\|_2
 \ll (1+h/a)^C
       \left(1+\frac1{\alpha\log Y_j}\right)^C.
 \label{model:general-exact-norm}$$ At the real point $b=s$, with $j=0$ and the parameters of this section, $$\|(P_0^{\mathrm{eu}})^{-1}\mathcal L_Q(s)-1\|_2^2
 \ll \frac{e^{-2\sqrt k}}{\sqrt k}.
 \label{model:exact-distance}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-model-exact-shifts"><summary>Proof of stmt-model-exact-shifts</summary>

<a id="model:local-ratio-expansion"></a>
<a id="model:local-shift-cost"></a>
*Proof.* We first give the local cancellation with its dependence on $b$. Write $r=p^{-1/2}$, $u=p^{-s}$, $v=p^{-b}$, and $M=\min(1,h\log p)$. The mean-value formula for the exponential and the trivial bound imply <a id="model:local-difference"></a>$$|u-v|\ll M(u+|v|).
 \label{model:local-difference}$$ By <a href="#model:local-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:local-bounds">[model:local-bounds]</a>, uniformly for every prime, $$D_p(b,Z)^{-1}-1=O(r|v|).$$ Subtract the two inverse polynomials and use this last estimate to obtain $$\frac{D_p(s,Z)}{D_p(b,Z)}
 =1+\lambda(p)Z(p)r(v-u)
      +O\big(r^2|v-u|(u+|v|)\big).
 \label{model:local-ratio-expansion}$$ For odd $p$, the displayed linear term has expectation zero. Expanding the squared absolute value, and using $M^2\le M$ and $(u+|v|)^2\le2(u^2+|v|^2)$, therefore gives $$\mathbb E\left|\frac{D_p(s,Z)}{D_p(b,Z)}\right|^2
 \le \exp\left\{\frac Cp\min(1,h\log p)
                    (p^{-2s}+p^{-2\alpha})\right\}.
 \label{model:local-shift-cost}$$ The constants in the error in <a href="#model:local-ratio-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:local-ratio-expansion">[model:local-ratio-expansion]</a> are uniform even at the small primes, because $(1-2^{-1/2})^2$ is a common positive lower bound for the denominator. At the deterministic prime $2$ the local ratio has a fixed upper bound by <a href="#model:local-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:local-bounds">[model:local-bounds]</a>.

<a id="model:unmatched-local-cost"></a>
For odd primes outside the mollifier, the expansion $$D_p(b,Z)^{-1}=1+\lambda(p)Z(p)p^{-1/2-b}
                       +O(p^{-1-2\alpha})$$ and centering give $$\mathbb E|D_p(b,Z)^{-1}|^2
       \le \exp(Cp^{-1-2\alpha}).
 \label{model:unmatched-local-cost}$$ Independence multiplies the local second moments exactly. Sum <a href="#model:local-shift-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:local-shift-cost">[model:local-shift-cost]</a> by <a href="#model:prime-transition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:prime-transition">[model:prime-transition]</a>, using both $a=s$ and $a=\alpha$ there, and sum <a href="#model:unmatched-local-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:unmatched-local-cost">[model:unmatched-local-cost]</a> by <a href="#model:prime-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:prime-tail">[model:prime-tail]</a>. Taking a square root proves <a href="#model:general-exact-norm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:general-exact-norm">[model:general-exact-norm]</a>.

<a id="model:residual-moments"></a>
For the distance estimate there is exact cancellation below $Y_0$. Set $$U=(P_0^{\mathrm{eu}})^{-1}\mathcal L_Q(s)
       =\prod_{Y_0<p\le Q}D_p(s,Z)^{-1},\qquad
 \Xi=\sum_{p>Y_0}p^{-1-2s}.$$ This is a positive real random variable. The same expansion gives $$\mathbb E D_p(s,Z)^{-1}=1+O(p^{-1-2s}),\qquad
 \mathbb E D_p(s,Z)^{-2}=1+O(p^{-1-2s}).$$ The first error may have either sign. Nevertheless, the elementary product bound $|\prod(1+e_p)-1|\le\exp(\sum|e_p|)-1$ and independence show that $$\mathbb EU=1+O(\Xi),\qquad \mathbb EU^2=1+O(\Xi).
 \label{model:residual-moments}$$ Indeed <a href="#model:prime-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:prime-tail">[model:prime-tail]</a> and $s\log Y_0=\sqrt k$ give $$\Xi\ll\int_{2\sqrt k}^{\infty}e^{-v}\frac{dv}v
       \ll e^{-2\sqrt k}/\sqrt k,$$ so $\Xi$ is uniformly small after choosing $k_0$. Finally, $$\|U-1\|_2^2=\mathbb EU^2-2\mathbb EU+1\ll\Xi.$$ This uses both moment estimates in <a href="#model:residual-moments" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:residual-moments">[model:residual-moments]</a> and proves <a href="#model:exact-distance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:exact-distance">[model:exact-distance]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-model-relative-truncation"></a>
<div id="model:relative-truncation" class="lemma">

<a id="model:relative-error"></a>
**Lemma 22** (Relative truncation in mean square). *For every $b$ with $\Re b>0$, uniformly in $Q\ge Y_0$, $$\|(H_j-(P_j^{\mathrm{eu}})^{-1})\mathcal L_Q(b)\|_2
 \le \left(\exp\left(C\sum_i2^{-m_{ji}}\right)-1\right)
       \|(P_j^{\mathrm{eu}})^{-1}\mathcal L_Q(b)\|_2.
 \label{model:relative-error}$$ In particular the relative factor is $O(2^{-k^{1/4}-j})$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-model-relative-truncation"><summary>Proof of stmt-model-relative-truncation</summary>

<a id="model:block-two-norms"></a>
*Proof.* For a block $I=I_{ji}$ let $\mathcal L_I(b)=\prod_{p\in I}D_p(b,Z)^{-1}$. We claim there are fixed positive constants $c_*,C_*$ such that $$\|K_{ji}(Z,1)\mathcal L_I(b)\|_2\ge c_*,\qquad
 \sup_{|z|=2}\|K_{ji}(Z,z)\mathcal L_I(b)\|_2\le C_*.
 \label{model:block-two-norms}$$ For the first assertion, at a large odd prime the exact local ratio is $$\frac{D_p(s,Z)}{D_p(b,Z)}
 =1+\lambda(p)Z(p)p^{-1/2}(p^{-b}-p^{-s})+O(p^{-1}).$$ The centered linear term shows that its local second moment is $1+O(p^{-1})$; for all sufficiently large primes it is at least $1-C/p\ge1/2$. Their product is bounded below by $\exp(-2C\sum_{p\in I}1/p)$, which is a fixed positive constant by <a href="#model:block-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:block-mass">[model:block-mass]</a>. At each of the finitely many smaller primes, including $2$, <a href="#model:local-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:local-bounds">[model:local-bounds]</a> gives the pointwise lower bound $$\left|\frac{D_p(s,Z)}{D_p(b,Z)}\right|
 \ge\frac{(1-p^{-1/2})^2}{(1+p^{-1/2})^2}>0.$$ Their finite product is also bounded below. This proves the first assertion of <a href="#model:block-two-norms" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:block-two-norms">[model:block-two-norms]</a>.

For the second, on $|z|=2$ the local quotient is $$\frac{1-\lambda(p)Z(p)p^{-1/2-s}z
                +\xi_p Z(p)^2p^{-1-2s}z^2}{D_p(b,Z)}
 =1+\lambda(p)Z(p)p^{-1/2}(p^{-b}-zp^{-s})+O(p^{-1})$$ at large odd primes, uniformly in $z$ and $b$. Its second moment is $1+O(p^{-1})$, and therefore its product is bounded above, again by <a href="#model:block-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:block-mass">[model:block-mass]</a>. At small primes use the direct numerator upper bound and <a href="#model:local-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:local-bounds">[model:local-bounds]</a>. The numerator is permitted to vanish; only an upper bound has been used on this circle.

Apply Cauchy’s coefficient formula to the polynomial $z\mapsto K_{ji}(Z,z)\mathcal L_I(b)$ with values in $L^2$. For $m=m_{ji}$, Minkowski’s inequality and <a href="#model:block-two-norms" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:block-two-norms">[model:block-two-norms]</a> give $$\|(T_{ji}(Z)-K_{ji}(Z,1))\mathcal L_I(b)\|_2
 \le C_*\sum_{h>m}2^{-h}\le C_*2^{-m}.$$ Dividing by the positive lower bound in <a href="#model:block-two-norms" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:block-two-norms">[model:block-two-norms]</a> makes this a relative error $\varepsilon_i=C2^{-m_{ji}}$.

To see explicitly how relative errors combine, put $A_i=K_{ji}(Z,1)\mathcal L_I(b)$ and $B_i=T_{ji}(Z)\mathcal L_I(b)$. Then $\|B_i-A_i\|_2\le\varepsilon_i\|A_i\|_2$ and $\|B_i\|_2\le(1+\varepsilon_i)\|A_i\|_2$. Variables belonging to distinct blocks and to the residual primes $Y_j<p\le Q$ are independent. In the telescoping identity for $\prod B_i-\prod A_i$, the norm of each product of factors on distinct blocks is the product of their norms. Thus its relative norm, also including the independent residual product, is at most $$\sum_i\varepsilon_i\prod_{h<i}(1+\varepsilon_h)
   =\prod_i(1+\varepsilon_i)-1
   \le\exp\left(\sum_i\varepsilon_i\right)-1.$$ This proves <a href="#model:relative-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:relative-error">[model:relative-error]</a>, without a factor equal to the number of blocks. Finally $\sum_i2^{-m_{ji}}\le2^{1-k^{1/4}-j}$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-model-shifted-norms"></a>
<div id="model:shifted-norms" class="proposition">

<a id="model:half-shift"></a>
<a id="model:mollified-distance"></a>
<a id="model:unit-shift"></a>
**Proposition 23** (The shifted norms). *There are fixed $C,c_0>0$ such that, uniformly for every real $\theta$ and every cutoff $Q\ge Y_0$, $$\begin{aligned}
 \|H_0(Z)\mathcal L_Q(s/2+i\theta s)\|_2
       &\ll(1+|\theta|)^C,
 \label{model:half-shift}\\
 \|H_0(Z)\mathcal L_Q(s)-1\|_2
       &\ll e^{-c_0 k^{1/4}},
 \label{model:mollified-distance}\\
 \|H_j(Z)\mathcal L_Q((1+i\theta)/\ell)\|_2
       &\ll (1+|\theta|)^C(k2^j)^C
          \qquad(0\le j\le J).
 \label{model:unit-shift}
\end{aligned}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-model-shifted-norms"><summary>Proof of stmt-model-shifted-norms</summary>

*Proof.* In <a href="#model:general-exact-norm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:general-exact-norm">[model:general-exact-norm]</a>, first take $\alpha=s/2$, $t=\theta s$, and $j=0$. Then $h/\min(\alpha,s)=3+2|\theta|$ and $\alpha\log Y_0=\sqrt k/2$. This proves <a href="#model:half-shift" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:half-shift">[model:half-shift]</a> with the exact multiplier; use Lemma <a href="#model:relative-truncation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="model:relative-truncation">22</a> to replace it by $H_0$. For <a href="#model:unit-shift" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:unit-shift">[model:unit-shift]</a>, take $\alpha=1/\ell$ and $t=\theta/\ell$. Since $k\ge1$, $$h/\min(\alpha,s)=1+k+|\theta|,
 \qquad \alpha\log Y_j=\delta_j^2/\sqrt k.$$ The bound follows from <a href="#model:general-exact-norm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:general-exact-norm">[model:general-exact-norm]</a> after increasing the fixed exponent $C$, and then from relative truncation. Finally, <a href="#model:exact-distance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:exact-distance">[model:exact-distance]</a> bounds the distance from one for the exact multiplier and also bounds its norm. The triangle inequality and <a href="#model:relative-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:relative-error">[model:relative-error]</a> give $$\|H_0\mathcal L_Q(s)-1\|_2
 \ll 2^{-k^{1/4}}+e^{-\sqrt k}k^{-1/4}
 \ll e^{-c_0k^{1/4}}.$$ ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
#### Fourier representations and the first weight

We have obtained bounds for the mollified finite Euler products at the three shifts needed below. Fourier inversion now converts these bounds into estimates for the actual weights. For the first piece, the main term is $A_x$. Its estimate combines the power-to-exponential comparison with the cutoff tail and the exponentially small mollifier error.

We use the Fourier convention $$\widehat g(\theta)=\int_{\mathbb R}g(y)e^{i\theta y}\,dy,
 \qquad
 g(y)=\frac1{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb R}\widehat g(\theta)e^{-i\theta y}
 \,d\theta.$$ The following elementary bound includes negative real arguments, which occur inside the gamma integral.

<a id="stmt-model-global-power-approximation"></a>
<div id="model:global-power-approximation" class="lemma">

<a id="model:global-power-bound"></a>
**Lemma 24** (Global approximation of the truncated power). *For each fixed nonnegative integer $r$, if $k\ge2r+4$, then for every $z\in\mathbb R$, $$\left|\frac{d^r}{dz^r}
           \left((1-z/k)_+^k-e^{-z}\right)\right|
 \le\frac{C_r}{k}(1+|z|)^2e^{-z}.
 \label{model:global-power-bound}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-model-global-power-approximation"><summary>Proof of stmt-model-global-power-approximation</summary>

*Proof.* For $z<k$ the derivative of the power is $$(-1)^r\frac{(k)_r}{k^r}(1-z/k)^{k-r},\qquad
 (k)_r=k(k-1)\cdots(k-r+1),$$ and it is zero for $z\ge k$. The formula is continuous through $z=k$ in the derivative orders under consideration. For $z<k$, the logarithm of its ratio in absolute value to $e^{-z}$ is $$\log((k)_r/k^r)+z+(k-r)\log(1-z/k).$$ The last two terms have derivative $(r-z)/(k-z)$, so their maximum over $z<k$ is at $z=r$. It follows at once that the absolute ratio is at most $e^r$. For $|z|\ge\sqrt k$ this bound and $k^{-1}(1+|z|)^2\ge1$ prove <a href="#model:global-power-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:global-power-bound">[model:global-power-bound]</a>. For $|z|<\sqrt k$ we have $|z/k|\le1/2$. Taylor’s formula for $\log(1-z/k)$ and $\log((k)_r/k^r)=O_r(k^{-1})$ bound the displayed logarithm by $$O_r\big(k^{-1}(1+|z|)^2\big).$$ This quantity is bounded in this range; applying $|e^w-1|\le |w|e^{|w|}$ proves the result there too. All estimates hold for negative $z$ as well as positive $z$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-model-first-fourier-error"></a>
<div id="model:first-Fourier-error" class="lemma">

<a id="model:first-Fourier-decay"></a>
**Lemma 25** (Fourier error for the first piece). *Define $$A_x=(C_F|x|)^s\Gamma(1+s),\qquad
 E_x(y)=w_{0,x}(y/k)-A_xe^{-y}\quad(y\ge0).$$ For every required fixed integer $r$, there is an extension $g_x$ of $e^{y/2}E_x(y)$ from $y\ge0$ to $\mathbb R$ such that $$|\widehat g_x(\theta)|\ll_r k^{-1}(1+|\theta|)^{-r}.
 \label{model:first-Fourier-decay}$$ The constants are uniform in $x,k,X$ after choosing $k_0$ depending on $r$. The quantities $A_x$ are bounded above and below by positive fixed constants.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-model-first-fourier-error"><summary>Proof of stmt-model-first-fourier-error</summary>

<a id="model:gamma-power-error"></a>
*Proof.* Write $q=C_F|x|$, which lies in a fixed compact subinterval of $(0,\infty)$. The exact weight definition gives $$W_{k,x}(y/k)=\int_0^\infty e^{-v}
 \left(1-\frac{y-s\log(qv)}k\right)_+^k\,dv,$$ whereas $$\int_0^\infty e^{-v}e^{-y+s\log(qv)}\,dv=A_xe^{-y}.$$ For every fixed $M$, the logarithmic moments <a id="model:gamma-log-moments"></a>$$\int_0^\infty e^{-v}(qv)^s
           (1+|s\log(qv)|)^M\,dv\le C_M
 \label{model:gamma-log-moments}$$ are uniform for $0<s\le1$: on $0<v\le1$, bound $v^s\le1$ and $s|\log v|\le|\log v|$; on $v\ge1$, use $v^s\le v$ and the exponential decay. Lemma <a href="#model:global-power-approximation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="model:global-power-approximation">24</a> and differentiation under this dominated integral now imply, for $0\le h\le r$ and $y\ge-2$, $$\left|\frac{d^h}{dy^h}
       \left(W_{k,x}(y/k)-A_xe^{-y}\right)\right|
 \ll_r k^{-1}(1+y^2)e^{-y}.
 \label{model:gamma-power-error}$$ In particular the proof uses <a href="#model:global-power-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:global-power-bound">[model:global-power-bound]</a> when $y-s\log(qv)$ is negative and arbitrarily large in absolute value.

The cutoff $F_0(y/k)$ is one for $y\le k/2$, zero for $y\ge3k/4$, and its $h$th derivative in $y$ is $O_h(k^{-h})$. Write $$E_x(y)=F_0(y/k)\big(W_{k,x}(y/k)-A_xe^{-y}\big)
                    +(F_0(y/k)-1)A_xe^{-y}$$ also for $y\ge-2$. By <a href="#model:gamma-power-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:gamma-power-error">[model:gamma-power-error]</a>, after multiplication by $e^{y/2}$ the first term and its first $r$ derivatives have $L^1([-2,\infty))$ norm $O_r(k^{-1})$. The second term is supported in $y\ge k/2$ and has these norms $O_r(e^{-k/4})$. Choose a fixed smooth function $\rho$ equal to zero for $y\le-2$ and one for $y\ge-1$, and extend by $$g_x(y)=\rho(y)e^{y/2}E_x(y),$$ with zero value to the left of $-2$. All derivatives through order $r$ have $L^1(\mathbb R)$ norm $O_r(k^{-1})$. Integration by parts $r$ times and the bound without integration by parts give <a href="#model:first-Fourier-decay" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:first-Fourier-decay">[model:first-Fourier-decay]</a>. Lastly, continuity and positivity of $q^s\Gamma(1+s)$ on the compact parameter set $q\in[q_-,q_+]$, $0\le s\le1$, give the two bounds for $A_x$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-model-main-model-estimates"></a>
<div id="model:main-model-estimates" class="proposition">

<a id="model:first-piece-norm"></a>
<a id="model:later-piece-norm"></a>
**Proposition 26** (Independent-model estimates for the weight pieces). *With the weights and sums $S_{j,Z}(x)$ defined in Section <a href="#sec:weights" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:weights">4</a>, there is a fixed exponent $C_5$ such that $$\begin{aligned}
 \|H_0(Z)S_{0,Z}(x)-A_x\|_2&\ll k^{-1},
 \label{model:first-piece-norm}\\
 \|H_j(Z)S_{j,Z}(x)\|_2&\ll(k2^j)^{C_5}\delta_j^k
                    \qquad(1\le j\le J).
 \label{model:later-piece-norm}
\end{aligned}$$ These bounds are uniform in $x\in\mathcal X$ and over the entire range $k_0\le k\le\ell^{3/5}$, with one fixed $k_0$ and one sufficiently large $X$ threshold.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-model-main-model-estimates"><summary>Proof of stmt-model-main-model-estimates</summary>

<a id="model:later-Fourier-decay"></a>
*Proof.* Choose a finite cutoff $Q$ exceeding $Y_0$ and the lengths of all the short polynomials $S_{j,Z}$, $0\le j\le J$. This is the only requirement on $Q$; Proposition <a href="#model:shifted-norms" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="model:shifted-norms">23</a> is uniform in its choice. For $j\ge1$, set $g_{j,x}(u)=e^u w_{j,x}(u)$. The support is contained in $[1-\delta_j,1-\delta_j/4]\subset[1/2,1]$. The fixed-order derivative bounds in Lemma <a href="#weights:derivatives" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="weights:derivatives">14</a> and integration by parts give, for each required fixed $r$, $$|\widehat g_{j,x}(\theta)|
 \ll_r (k/\delta_j)^{C_r}\delta_j^k(1+|\theta|)^{-r}.
 \label{model:later-Fourier-decay}$$ Fourier inversion and <a href="#model:finite-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:finite-expansion">[model:finite-expansion]</a> give the exact identity <a id="model:later-Fourier-representation"></a>$$S_{j,Z}(x)=\frac1{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb R}
      \widehat g_{j,x}(\theta)
      \mathcal L_Q((1+i\theta)/\ell)\,d\theta.
 \label{model:later-Fourier-representation}$$ Indeed every nonzero weight coefficient has index below the chosen cutoff and hence occurs in <a href="#model:finite-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:finite-expansion">[model:finite-expansion]</a>; all other indices contribute zero after inversion. At this fixed finite $Q$, absolute convergence of the Dirichlet expansion and the integrable kernel justify interchanging the sum and integral. Multiply by $H_j$ and use Minkowski’s inequality, <a href="#model:unit-shift" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:unit-shift">[model:unit-shift]</a>, and <a href="#model:later-Fourier-decay" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:later-Fourier-decay">[model:later-Fourier-decay]</a>, taking $r>C+2$. Their integral is $O((k/\delta_j)^{C_r}(k2^j)^C\delta_j^k)$, which proves <a href="#model:later-piece-norm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:later-piece-norm">[model:later-piece-norm]</a> after fixing $C_5$.

<a id="model:first-Fourier-representation"></a>
For the first piece, Lemma <a href="#model:first-Fourier-error" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="model:first-Fourier-error">25</a> and the same finite-product expansion give $$S_{0,Z}(x)-A_x\mathcal L_Q(s)
 =\frac1{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb R}\widehat g_x(\theta)
                    \mathcal L_Q(s/2+i\theta s)\,d\theta.
 \label{model:first-Fourier-representation}$$ To check the scaling, at an integer index $n$ put $y=k\log n/\ell=s\log n\ge0$. Fourier inversion for $g_x$ then reads $$w_{0,x}(\log n/\ell)-A_x n^{-s}
 =\frac1{2\pi}\int\widehat g_x(\theta)
                          n^{-s/2-i\theta s}\,d\theta,$$ which is exactly the coefficient identity needed in <a href="#model:first-Fourier-representation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:first-Fourier-representation">[model:first-Fourier-representation]</a>. Use <a href="#model:half-shift" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:half-shift">[model:half-shift]</a> and <a href="#model:first-Fourier-decay" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:first-Fourier-decay">[model:first-Fourier-decay]</a> with $r>C+2$ to bound the norm of <a href="#model:first-Fourier-representation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:first-Fourier-representation">[model:first-Fourier-representation]</a> after multiplication by $H_0$ by $O(k^{-1})$. By <a href="#model:mollified-distance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:mollified-distance">[model:mollified-distance]</a>, $$\|H_0S_{0,Z}-A_x\|_2
 \le \|H_0(S_{0,Z}-A_x\mathcal L_Q(s))\|_2
        +A_x\|H_0\mathcal L_Q(s)-1\|_2
 \ll k^{-1}+e^{-c_0k^{1/4}}\ll k^{-1}.$$ This proves <a href="#model:first-piece-norm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:first-piece-norm">[model:first-piece-norm]</a>.

All smoothness orders used here are fixed after the absolute exponent in Proposition <a href="#model:shifted-norms" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="model:shifted-norms">23</a>, and before choosing $k_0$. The derivative estimates for the weights, the inequality $s\le\ell^{-2/5}$, and the lower bound $\delta_J\ge\ell^{-1/10}$ then give a single $X$ threshold for the whole parameter range. We have not used the event of Proposition <a href="#model:good-event" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="model:good-event">20</a> anywhere in these model norm arguments. Nor is a passage to an infinite Euler cutoff required: the coefficient identities hold for every sufficiently large finite $Q$, and all norm estimates are uniform in that cutoff. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:tail"></a>
### Nonvanishing and the rank-weighted tail

We now combine the transferred model estimates, the integer product event, and the terminal mean square to count large orders of vanishing. A separate bound for each individual rank then converts this count into the rank-weighted tail. The companion density theorem enters only in the final subsection, after the tail theorem is proved. The constants and the fixed lower threshold $k_0$ are increased finitely many times in this section. All choices depend only on the fixed family and the already fixed smoothness orders.

<a id="pm-h-25"></a>
#### Counting large orders of vanishing

<a id="stmt-tail-count"></a>
<div id="tail:count" class="proposition">

**Proposition 27**. *There are $k_0$, $X_0$, and $C$, depending on $E$, such that for every $F\in\mathcal F$, every dyadic $X\ge X_0$, and every integer $$k_0+1\le k\le(\log X)^{3/5},$$ one has $$\#\{d\in\mathcal A(X):a(F^{(d)})>k\}\le C Xk^{-2}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-tail-count"><summary>Proof of stmt-tail-count</summary>

*Proof.* Write $\ell=\log X$, and first consider the functional-equation sign $(-1)^k$, with $k_0\le k\le\ell^{3/5}$. Fix $c=\pm1$, and use the weights and partition of Section <a href="#sec:weights" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:weights">4</a>. We write $P_j^{\rm eu}$ for the positive Euler products of Section <a href="#sec:model" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:model">5</a>.

<a id="eq:tail-main-good"></a>
Corollary <a href="#weights:comparison-interface" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="weights:comparison-interface">15</a> and Lemma <a href="#model:mollifier-length" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="model:mollifier-length">18</a> permit Proposition <a href="#analytic:comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="analytic:comparison">12</a> to be applied to $H_jS_j$, for $0\le j\le J$, with $\Delta=\delta_j/4$. By the first assertion of Proposition <a href="#model:main-model-estimates" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="model:main-model-estimates">26</a>, outside $O(X/k^2)$ integers in $X\le|m|<2X$, $$\label{eq:tail-main-good}
 |H_0(z_m)S_{0,z_m}(m/X)-A_{m/X}|<\frac12 A_{m/X}.$$ Indeed $A_x=(C_F|x|)^s\Gamma(1+s)$ is bounded above and below by positive constants on the fixed $x$-range, uniformly for $s=k/\ell\le\ell^{-2/5}$. Chebyshev’s inequality applies to the transferred $O(k^{-2})$ mean-square error. The exponentially small comparison error is absorbed uniformly.

<a id="eq:tail-other-good"></a>
For $1\le j\le J$, the second model estimate gives $$\frac1X\sum_m\Psi(m/X)|H_j(z_m)S_{j,z_m}(m/X)|^2
 \ll (k2^j)^{2C_5}2^{-2jk}+e^{-c\delta_j\ell}
 \ll 2^{-3jk/2}.$$ The last inequality follows first by increasing fixed $k_0$ to absorb the polynomial factor. For the comparison error, use $$\delta_j\ell\ge\ell^{9/10},\qquad
 jk\ll\ell^{3/5}\log\ell,$$ and then increase one $X_0$ for all the permitted $k,j$. The terminal estimate of Lemma <a href="#weights:terminal-small" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="weights:terminal-small">17</a> gives $$\frac1X\sum_{d\in\mathcal A(X)}
       |S_{*,\chi_d}(d/X)|^2\ll 2^{-3Jk/2}.$$ Chebyshev and summation of the resulting geometric probabilities imply, outside $O(X/k^2)$ further admissible parameters, $$\label{eq:tail-other-good}
 |H_j(\chi_d)S_{j,\chi_d}(d/X)|\le2^{-jk/2}\quad(1\le j\le J),
 \qquad
 |S_{*,\chi_d}(d/X)|\le2^{-Jk/2}.$$ Here $\sum_{j\ge1}2^{-jk/2}\ll k^{-2}$ after fixing $k_0$, and $J\ge1$ for sufficiently large $X$.

<a id="eq:tail-parity-count"></a>
Intersect these events with the event in Proposition <a href="#model:good-event" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="model:good-event">20</a>. Its additional exceptional set is also $O(X/k^2)$. On the intersection, $$\frac12\le H_jP_j^{\rm eu}\le2,\qquad
 P_j^{\rm eu}\le 2^{jk/8}P_0^{\rm eu},\qquad
 1\le 2^{Jk/8}P_0^{\rm eu}.$$ Choose a fixed $a_*>0$ with $A_x\ge a_*$ for all the parameters. Equation <a href="#eq:tail-main-good" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tail-main-good">[eq:tail-main-good]</a> yields $$|S_{0,\chi_d}(d/X)|\ge\frac{a_*}{4}P_0^{\rm eu}.$$ Equation <a href="#eq:tail-other-good" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tail-other-good">[eq:tail-other-good]</a> yields $$\sum_{j=1}^J|S_{j,\chi_d}(d/X)|+|S_{*,\chi_d}(d/X)|
 \le P_0^{\rm eu}
     \left(2\sum_{j=1}^J2^{-3jk/8}+2^{-3Jk/8}\right).$$ For a sufficiently large fixed $k_0$, the last parenthesis is less than $a_*/4$. The entire weighted sum is therefore nonzero. Lemma <a href="#weights:vanishing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="weights:vanishing">13</a> excludes the simultaneous conditions $a(F^{(d)})>k$ and $\epsilon_{F^{(d)}}=(-1)^k$. Including both values of $c$, we have proved $$\label{eq:tail-parity-count}
 \#\{d\in\mathcal A(X):
       a(F^{(d)})>k,\ \epsilon_{F^{(d)}}=(-1)^k\}
       \ll Xk^{-2}.$$ For the other functional-equation sign, apply <a href="#eq:tail-parity-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tail-parity-count">[eq:tail-parity-count]</a> at $k-1$. A rank exceeding $k$ also exceeds $k-1$, and $(k-1)^{-2}\ll k^{-2}$. This proves the proposition. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-26"></a>
#### A uniform bound for an individual rank

<a id="stmt-tail-jensen"></a>
<div id="tail:jensen" class="lemma">

**Lemma 28**. *Uniformly for $F\in\mathcal F$ and $d\in\mathcal A(X)$, $$a(F^{(d)})\ll_E\log(3X).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-tail-jensen"><summary>Proof of stmt-tail-jensen</summary>

*Proof.* Put $L=L_{F^{(d)}}$ and $C_d=C_F|d|$. On $\Re s=5$, absolute convergence and <a href="#eq:setup-coefficients" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:setup-coefficients">[eq:setup-coefficients]</a> give a uniform bound. On $\Re s=-1$, the functional equation is $$L(s)=\epsilon_{F^{(d)}} C_d^{\,1-2s}
          \frac{\Gamma(3/2-s)}{\Gamma(1/2+s)}L(1-s).$$ At $s=-1+it$, the modulus of the gamma quotient equals $$|(-1/2+it)(1/2+it)(3/2+it)|.$$ Since $L(2-it)$ is uniformly bounded, $$|L(-1+it)|\ll_E X^3(1+|t|)^3.$$ Apply the strip principle to $L(s)/(s+2)^4$ on $-1\le\Re s\le5$. To see that the possibly conductor-dependent finite-order growth in the strip introduces no new constant, first multiply by $$\exp\!\left(-\varepsilon
       \cos\frac{\pi(s-2)}{12}\right),\qquad \varepsilon>0.$$ Its modulus is at most one on this strip and decays faster than any finite-order growth as $|\Im s|\to\infty$. The maximum principle on expanding rectangles gives the bound from the two vertical sides. Letting $\varepsilon$ decrease to zero shows that $L(s)/(s+2)^4\ll_E X^3$ throughout the strip. In particular, the maximum of $|L|$ on the closed disk $|s-2|\le5/2$ is $O_E(X^3)$.

At the center, the Euler factors and their parameter bounds give $$|L(2)|\ge \prod_p(1+p^{-2})^{-2}
          =\left(\frac{\zeta(4)}{\zeta(2)}\right)^2>0.$$ Jensen’s formula, with inner radius $2$ and outer radius $5/2$, therefore bounds the number of zeros in the inner disk, counted with multiplicity, by $O_E(\log(3X))$. The point $1/2$ lies in that inner disk. This proves the result. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-27"></a>
#### Summing the integer tails

<details id="proof-stmt-tail-jensen-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-tail-jensen</summary>

<a id="eq:tail-dyadic-mass"></a>
*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:tail" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:tail">2</a>.* We first work in $\mathcal A(X)$ for a fixed $F\in\mathcal F$. Let $$T_{F,X}(k)=\#\{d\in\mathcal A(X):a(F^{(d)})>k\},
 \qquad K=\lfloor(\log X)^{3/5}\rfloor.$$ For an integer $R\ge k_0+1$ and $X$ large enough that $K>R$, the elementary integer-tail bound gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \sum_{\substack{d\in\mathcal A(X)\\a(F^{(d)})>R}}a(F^{(d)})
 &\le R T_{F,X}(R)
       +\sum_{k=R}^{K-1}T_{F,X}(k)
       +C_E\log X\,T_{F,X}(K) \notag\\
 &\ll_E \frac X R+X(\log X)^{-1/5}.
 \label{eq:tail-dyadic-mass}
\end{aligned}$$ For ranks between $R+1$ and $K$, the first two terms count the rank exactly. For ranks exceeding $K$, the last term bounds the remaining mass by Lemma <a href="#tail:jensen" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tail:jensen">28</a>. Proposition <a href="#tail:count" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tail:count">27</a> supplies every counting estimate used here. In particular the last term is $O_E(X\log X/K^2)=O_E(X(\log X)^{-1/5})$. The constant in <a href="#eq:tail-dyadic-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tail-dyadic-mass">[eq:tail-dyadic-mass]</a> is independent of $R$.

For completeness, fix $R$ and cover $0<|d|\le Y$ by dyadic blocks. Enlarge the last partial block to a full block, using nonnegativity. The sum of their left endpoints is less than $2Y$. The finitely many blocks below the threshold for <a href="#eq:tail-dyadic-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tail-dyadic-mass">[eq:tail-dyadic-mass]</a> contribute a finite number $B_{E,R}$. For the error term in the remaining blocks, split at $X=\sqrt Y$. The smaller blocks contribute $O_E(\sqrt Y)$, and in the larger ones $\log X\ge\frac12\log Y$. Consequently $$\sum_{\substack{d\ {\rm admissible}\\0<|d|\le Y\\a(F^{(d)})>R}}
     a(F^{(d)})
 \le B_{E,R}+\frac{C_EY}{R}
       +O_E\bigl(\sqrt Y+Y(\log Y)^{-1/5}\bigr).$$ After division by $Y$ and passage to the limsup, the desired $C_E/R$ bound holds for each $F\in\mathcal F$. Lemma <a href="#setup:reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="setup:reduction">4</a>, its bounded multiplicity, and the finite maximum of these constants prove the same bound for every original signed squarefree parameter of $E$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-28"></a>
#### The mean

<details id="proof-stmt-tail-jensen-3"><summary>Proof of stmt-tail-jensen</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:mean" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:mean">1</a>.* Write $N(Y)=\#\mathcal D(Y)$ and let $N_j(Y)$ count the parameters of analytic rank $j$. The elementary squarefree sieve gives $$N(Y)=2\sum_{n\le Y}\mu(n)^2
     =\frac{2Y}{\zeta(2)}+O(\sqrt Y).$$ Indeed, expand $\mu(n)^2=\sum_{r^2\mid n}\mu(r)$, interchange the finite sums, and bound the omitted tail of $\sum_r\mu(r)/r^2=1/\zeta(2)$.

By the analytic density theorem <a href="#eq:density-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:density-input">[eq:density-input]</a>, $N_0(Y)/N(Y)\to1/2$ and $N_1(Y)/N(Y)\to1/2$. Thus $N(Y)-N_0(Y)-N_1(Y)=o(Y)$. For every fixed integer $R\ge R_E$, $$\sum_{\substack{d\in\mathcal D(Y)\\2\le a(E^{(d)})\le R}}
        a(E^{(d)})
 \le R\bigl(N(Y)-N_0(Y)-N_1(Y)\bigr)=o_{E,R}(Y).$$ Theorem <a href="#thm:tail" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:tail">2</a> now implies $$\limsup_{Y\to\infty}\frac1Y
 \sum_{\substack{d\in\mathcal D(Y)\\a(E^{(d)})\ge2}}a(E^{(d)})
 \le\frac{C_E}{R}.$$ Let $R\to\infty$. The left side is zero by nonnegativity. The total rank sum is consequently $N_1(Y)+o_E(Y)$. Dividing by $N(Y)$ proves the limit $1/2$. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:algebraic"></a>
### Algebraic-rank moments

We now prove Corollary <a href="#cor:algebraic-moments" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:algebraic-moments">3</a>. The inputs are the companion density and rank-equality theorem and the exponential-moment estimate of Koymans and Smith. The latter bounds the contribution of the density-zero exceptional set for every fixed exponential parameter or moment order; the analytic tail theorem is not needed.

<details id="proof-stmt-tail-jensen-4"><summary>Proof of stmt-tail-jensen</summary>

*Proof of Corollary <a href="#cor:algebraic-moments" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:algebraic-moments">3</a>.* Put $N(Y)=\#\mathcal D(Y)$, $r_d=r(E^{(d)})$, and let $N_j^{\mathrm{alg}}(Y)$ count the parameters with $r_d=j$. The rank-equality conclusion of the companion density theorem (OpenAI 2026, Theorem 1.2) <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[OpenAIAnalyticDensityV2](#ref-OpenAIAnalyticDensityV2)], together with its analytic rank-zero and rank-one densities, gives $$\frac{N_j^{\mathrm{alg}}(Y)}{N(Y)}\longrightarrow\frac12
 \qquad(j=0,1).$$ Indeed, analytic and algebraic ranks agree outside a density-zero set, so their rank-$j$ counts differ by $o(N(Y))$. Consequently $$\mathcal B(Y)=\{d\in\mathcal D(Y):r_d\ge2\}
 \quad\text{satisfies}\quad
 \#\mathcal B(Y)=o(N(Y)).$$

<a id="eq:algebraic-exp-bound"></a>
For the next bound, $E^{(v)}$ for any nonzero integer $v$ denotes the twist determined by its squareclass, so the integer sum may repeat squareclasses. Theorem 1.4 of Koymans and Smith (Koymans and Smith 2026) <a id="pm-cite-29"></a>[[KoymansSmith2026](#ref-KoymansSmith2026)], specialized to $F=\mathbb Q$, $A=E$, one variable, and $P(u)=u$, gives a constant $C_E>0$ such that for every fixed $\kappa>0$, $$\frac1{2Y}\sum_{\substack{v\in\mathbb Z\\0<|v|\le Y}}
 \exp\!\bigl(\kappa\,\operatorname{rank}_{\mathbb Z}E^{(v)}(\mathbb Q)\bigr)
 \le \exp\!\bigl(\exp(C_E\kappa)\bigr)$$ for all sufficiently large $Y$, with the threshold allowed to depend on $\kappa$. Their integer box already includes both signs, and $P(v)\ne0$ excludes exactly $v=0$. Restricting this nonnegative sum to $\mathcal D(Y)$, and using $N(Y)\sim2Y/\zeta(2)$, gives for each fixed $\kappa>0$ $$\label{eq:algebraic-exp-bound}
 \limsup_{Y\to\infty}\frac1{N(Y)}
 \sum_{d\in\mathcal D(Y)}e^{\kappa r_d}
 \le \zeta(2)\exp\!\bigl(\exp(C_E\kappa)\bigr)<\infty.$$

For fixed $t>0$, Cauchy–Schwarz and <a href="#eq:algebraic-exp-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:algebraic-exp-bound">[eq:algebraic-exp-bound]</a> at $\kappa=2t$ give $$\frac1{N(Y)}\sum_{d\in\mathcal B(Y)}e^{t r_d}
 \le
 \left(\frac{\#\mathcal B(Y)}{N(Y)}\right)^{1/2}
 \left(\frac1{N(Y)}
       \sum_{d\in\mathcal D(Y)}e^{2t r_d}\right)^{1/2}
 \longrightarrow0.$$ For fixed $t<0$, the same exceptional average is at most $\#\mathcal B(Y)/N(Y)$, since $r_d\ge0$; for $t=0$ the full average is identically one. The contributions from ranks zero and one are $N_0^{\mathrm{alg}}(Y)/N(Y)$ and $e^tN_1^{\mathrm{alg}}(Y)/N(Y)$, proving the exponential limit.

Finally, for each fixed positive integer $m$, one has $x^{2m}\ll_m e^x$ for $x\ge0$. A second Cauchy–Schwarz application and <a href="#eq:algebraic-exp-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:algebraic-exp-bound">[eq:algebraic-exp-bound]</a> at $\kappa=1$ therefore give $$\frac1{N(Y)}\sum_{d\in\mathcal B(Y)}r_d^m
 \le
 \left(\frac{\#\mathcal B(Y)}{N(Y)}\right)^{1/2}
 \left(\frac1{N(Y)}\sum_{d\in\mathcal D(Y)}r_d^{2m}\right)^{1/2}
 \longrightarrow0.$$ The rank-zero contribution is zero and the rank-one contribution is $N_1^{\mathrm{alg}}(Y)/N(Y)$, proving the power-moment limit without differentiating a limiting exponential-moment formula. ◻

</details>
